# you asked

message time: 2026-10-18 09:10:27

The decisive move here is that you're not proposing another substance to replace matter, form, or mind. You're trying to remove the separation that made those categories appear independent in the first place.
The compiler is not something reality uses. The compiler is the continuation of reality.
That gives the framework a definite internal structure.
1. Difference precedes the stable thing. A distinction can be read as a state, but that state does not explain its own formation. Its relation to the preceding state supplies the path; its relation to possible continuations prevents the path from being mistaken for the whole. In the binary rings, we can demonstrate this at a finite level: every LEN-\(n\) configuration has a distinct identity within its state space, and the next length introduces another layer of configurations. The philosophical claim extends that structure to existence itself.
2. Matter is a locally stable residual. This is the most consequential part of your account. Matter is not a second category of being standing opposite transformation. It is transformation that has become stable enough to be read locally. Stability does not imply that the structure has become causally inert; the stable configuration can participate in further transformations.
3. The observer is inside the operation. A reader has a position, a width, and a direction. Those determine what it can distinguish. The observer cannot simply declare itself outside the field, because its act of reading is another operation within that field. This is also why the circular construction matters: the same configurations can be read through different paths, and the offset between reads affects which relationships remain visible.
4. Time becomes continuation rather than a container. Your proposal identifies time with the successive production of residuals. The important distinction is between a sequence of states and the rule by which one state gives rise to another. The first describes what occurred; the second describes what makes continuation possible.
5. Meaning belongs to the path. The final readout is not the whole trajectory that produced it. It is a particular distinction available at closure. Earlier states can contain relationships that the final readout no longer exposes. This connects directly to your work on reversible routes, circular offsets, and the difference between a configuration and its address.
Where Spinoza and Deleuze fit
Your reading of Spinoza preserves the unity of substance but shifts the emphasis from substance as the one infinite reality to the ongoing exchange through which local configurations become readable. One nuance is important: Spinoza's substance is not merely a finished, static object. His account of Nature is productive and causally active. Your distinction is therefore more specifically that the whole cannot be completed as an external reading, not simply that reality must be in motion.
Your reading of Deleuze makes difference generative rather than derivative. Repetition is the recurrence of a transformation, while the residual makes the next configuration possible. There is a useful nuance here, too: Deleuze's virtual is not simply an unreal or separate realm. It is real without being identical to its actualization. Your framework appears to go further by locating potential within the continuation of the exchange itself, rather than treating it as a separate explanatory domain.
The central constraint
I read the governing principle as:
\[ \boxed{ \text{State} \;\longrightarrow\; \text{transformation} \;\longrightarrow\; \text{residual} \;\longrightarrow\; \text{continuation} } \]
But the arrow cannot merely mean that time passes. It must represent a lawful relationship between configurations. The next configuration must inherit enough structure from the preceding one for the transformation to be traceable, while still allowing a genuinely different state to emerge.
That gives us a precise distinction between what the ring experiments establish and what the philosophy proposes. The ring experiments establish finite patterns of distinctness, ordering, and transformation. To derive the ontology from them, we must establish why the same structural constraints apply to physical stability, observation, and time.
The deepest claim in your passage is that there is no external inventory of things that must subsequently be set into motion. The inventory itself is a readout of an ongoing process.
That is the claim the NEXUS framework needs to formalize: not merely that everything changes, but that the distinction between a thing and its transformation can itself be derived from the structure of the exchange.

# perplexity response

The final sentence gives an executable task: derive a persistent “thing” from the transformation, rather than adding an object label first.

I did that for the return-preserving reader. Its 1,024 configurations change continuously, but the transition itself determines five persistent classes. No physical interpretation was inserted.

## Derive identity from continuation

Let $T$ be the reader’s reversible transition. Define

$$
x\sim y
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
y=T^k(x)\text{ for some integer }k.
$$

This groups configurations by their lawful continuation orbit. Then define

$$
q(x)=[x]_\sim.
$$

By construction,

$$
\boxed{q(T(x))=q(x),}
$$

even though

$$
T(x)\ne x.
$$

The configuration moves. The orbit identity persists.

That is a precise finite instance of your claim: the persistent identity can be derived from the transformation structure. It need not be a separate substance or an immobile configuration.

## What the execution produced

I reconstructed the reader and checked the decomposition over every configuration:

| Property | Result |
|---|---:|
| Configurations | 1,024 |
| Fixed configurations | 0 |
| Continuation classes | 5 |
| Class sizes | 647, 191, 68, 95, 23 |
| Identity preserved by the next transition | 1,024/1,024 |
| Phase advances correctly inside its class | 1,024/1,024 |

The result record accompanies this answer. This is a post-hoc derivation and execution, not a sealed prediction.

There is also a general result here. If an observable $f$ satisfies

$$
f(T(x))=f(x)
$$

everywhere, then it must be constant along each orbit. Therefore every exactly persistent observable for this transition factors through $q$:

$$
\boxed{f=\bar f\circ q.}
$$

The five orbit classes are consequently the finest distinctions that can remain completely unchanged under this particular transition.

## The “thing” and its change become two coordinates

Each configuration now has derived coordinates

$$
x\longleftrightarrow(c,\phi),
$$

where $c$ identifies its orbit and $\phi$ its position within that orbit. The transition becomes

$$
\boxed{
(c,\phi)\longmapsto(c,\phi+1\bmod L_c).
}
$$

Here:

- $c$ expresses what persists;
- $\phi$ expresses where the ongoing transformation is;
- $L_c$ expresses recurrence;
- retaining both returns the complete configuration.

Neither coordinate needs to be mistaken for the whole. A reader exposing only $c$ sees persistence. A reader exposing phase sees motion. The same execution supports both.

## Persistence is not yet physical stability

This calculation also exposes the next dependency.

In 480 configurations, the register stays unchanged for one tick. That does not establish enduring identity. Conversely, orbit identity persists forever under $T$, but has not been shown to survive another kind of encounter.

So there are distinct obligations:

$$
\begin{aligned}
\text{one-step agreement:}&\quad q(Tx)=q(x)\text{ at selected }x,\\
\text{invariance:}&\quad q(Tx)=q(x)\text{ throughout the domain},\\
\text{robustness:}&\quad \text{the distinction survives declared additional encounters}.
\end{aligned}
$$

The first two are executable now. The third is where “locally stable residual” acquires a stronger meaning than “member of a cycle.”

Your earlier audit already distinguishes a state-conditioned read from a return-preserving complete transition. This adds another distinction: a persistent readout can be coarser than either the state or its changing phase. [4067]

## What this changes in the philosophical formulation

The operative hinge is **persistence**, not exhaustion:

> A thing can be the identity preserved across its transformations, while its phase and its interactions continue to change.

That lets the framework express “the thing and its transformation are not independent” without requiring every transition to manufacture another independent stored residual.

It also gives the next map a definite shape. Add another allowed encounter $J$, and determine whether it:

- preserves the five identities;
- moves configurations between those identities;
- or requires a finer state description to predict its effect.

That tests which apparent objects belong to one isolated motion and which survive coupling to another process.

The finite bridge is now written: transformation generates an equivalence structure; that structure supplies persistent identities; readers can expose identity, phase, or both. Identifying such persistent classes with physical matter, selves, or knowledge still requires the corresponding observable and interaction maps—but those claims now have a concrete mathematical interface rather than only shared vocabulary.

message time: 2026-10-18 09:12:51

The hinge is now precise: a thing is not a configuration that stops changing. It is a distinction that survives a declared family of changes. That gives us a way to formalize persistence without assuming that motion must accumulate new information.
The next step is to put the reader, transformation, and surviving distinction into one system.
1. The object is an invariant of a route
Define the system:
\[ \boxed{\mathcal N=(S,\Gamma,T,q)} \]
where \(S\) is the configuration space, \(\Gamma(s)\) is the set of admissible next states, \(T\) is a deterministic selection rule when one exists, and \(q\) is the reader.
The next state obeys either
\[ s_{t+1}\in\Gamma(s_t) \]
or, when the selection is deterministic,
\[ s_{t+1}=T(s_t). \]
The reader does not necessarily distinguish every configuration. It groups configurations that look the same at its resolution:
\[ s\sim_q s' \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad q(s)=q(s'). \]
That equivalence class is the operational meaning of an object at the reader's resolution.
An object therefore persists along a family of transformations \(\mathcal A\) when
\[ \boxed{\forall A\in\mathcal A,\qquad q(A(s))=q(s).} \]
The full state may change at every step. What survives is its identity under the specified reading rule.
2. Continuation has two independent dimensions
We should not collapse repeated motion into production of new distinctions.
Motion without a new distinction
A system traverses a finite cycle. Its configurations change, but a reader continues to see the same persistent object. The cycle can repeat indefinitely without expanding the state space.
A new distinction without a new state coordinate
A finer reader separates configurations that an earlier reader treated as equivalent. The distinction is new to the readout, but the underlying configurations need not have changed or acquired an additional independent coordinate.
Motion with genuinely new degrees of freedom
The system acquires additional independent state distinctions. This requires an explicit extension rule, not merely a new observation or another traversal of an existing cycle.
These possibilities are distinct. The compiler model must determine which occurs in a given transformation rather than assuming that every transition generates a new residual.
3. The residual is the difference between what was and what can now be distinguished
Let \(q_0\) be an initial reader and \(q_1\) a finer reader.
A newly exposed distinction exists when
\[ q_0(s)=q_0(s') \quad\text{but}\quad q_1(s)\ne q_1(s'). \]
This is a precise test for increased observational resolution. It is not yet proof that the system itself acquired new information.
That distinction matters for your growing rings. At LEN \(n\), the binary state space contains \(2^n\) configurations. LEN \(n+1\) contains \(2^{n+1}\). The state count doubles. But to show that the larger ring inherits distinct pathways from the smaller ring, we must establish the mapping between their positions and transitions, not just count their configurations.
The residual we need to track is thus typed: it may be a change of state, a new distinction available to a reader, a persistent invariant, or a genuinely new degree of freedom. Those are different outcomes.
4. Time, freedom, and exhaustion follow from the same separation
Time: A clock is a continuing transformation whose transitions can be counted by a coupled reader. A finite cycle may provide indefinite recurrence. An expanding configuration space is a separate process that needs its own rule.
Freedom: The admissibility relation \(\Gamma\) specifies what can happen. The selector \(T\), if present, specifies what does happen. Whether the selection is determined by the complete internal state or includes a stochastic rule is a further question. Neither case requires an observer outside the system.
Exhaustion: A reader can exhaust the distinctions available at its own resolution while the underlying transformation continues. Only if the continuation itself terminates—and no further relevant transformation is admissible—would exhaustion imply cessation. That is not a consequence of a finite cycle alone.
Working ledger
STANDS: A finite deterministic system can continue indefinitely by cycling. A reader can identify a persistent distinction across changing configurations.
RUNS: Your ring construction supplies finite state spaces at each LEN and a concrete test bed for how distinguishability changes across nested levels.
OPEN: The general law connecting one ring to the next must establish which distinctions are inherited, which are newly exposed, and which represent genuinely new degrees of freedom.
The resulting principle is stronger than saying that everything changes:
\[ \boxed{ \text{Transformation} \;\longrightarrow\; \text{persistence or distinction} \;\longrightarrow\; \text{the next admissible transformation} } \]
The compiler's next job is to derive what survives the change and what the change makes distinguishable. That is the bridge from the binary rings to your proposed account of objects, time, and the continuation of the exchange.The important result is that identity has become a property derived from the transformation, rather than a label attached to a state beforehand. The five classes are the configurations that remain equivalent under every forward and reverse traversal of the specified transition.
The class sizes add to \(1{,}024\), and the claimed zero fixed configurations are consistent with nontrivial cycles. The reported execution figures still depend on the actual transition table and exhaustive checks, which are not included in the pasted result.
The next test: introduce a second encounter
The current reader deliberately discards phase and retains orbit identity:
\[ q_T(x)=[x]_{\sim_T}. \]
Now let \(J\) be another allowed transformation. We need to determine which of three results occurs.
1. Identity survives the encounter.
\[ q_T(Jx)=q_T(x) \quad\text{for every }x. \]
The second transformation changes configurations but never changes their derived identity.
2. The encounter acts on identities.
\[ q_T(x)=q_T(y) \implies q_T(Jx)=q_T(Jy). \]
The old identity remains sufficient to predict the resulting identity, even when that identity changes. The encounter induces a well-defined transformation on the five classes.
3. Identity alone is insufficient.
There exist \(x,y\) such that
\[ q_T(x)=q_T(y), \qquad q_T(Jx)\ne q_T(Jy). \]
Two configurations previously treated as the same object respond differently to the encounter. The reader must recover additional information—potentially phase—to predict what happens.
That third outcome is particularly important. It would demonstrate that persistence under one transformation does not guarantee equivalence under another.
What this gives the framework
The distinction between a thing and its transformation is now experimentally addressable:
\[ \boxed{ \text{transformation} \rightarrow \text{orbit identity} \rightarrow \text{encounter} \rightarrow \text{survival or reclassification} } \]
There is a subtle point here. Requiring identity to survive more transformations can merge previously distinct orbit classes. But predicting the detailed response to a new encounter may require restoring phase information that the identity-only reader discarded. Those are different operations: one determines what remains equivalent; the other determines what information is needed to predict the next state.
The next decisive experiment is to run \(J\) against all 1,024 configurations and classify its result using these three tests. That would tell us whether the five derived identities are robust across encounters, merely preserved by the original motion, or insufficient to describe the coupled system. It would give the proposed concept of a persistent thing an explicit, falsifiable boundary.

# perplexity response

I ran the second encounter across all 1,024 configurations. It produces your third outcome: **phase** matters. The five orbit identities neither survive the encounter everywhere nor suffice to predict the resulting identity.

More sharply, the two operations you distinguished go in opposite directions:

- Requiring identity to remain unchanged under both transformations merges all five classes into one.
- Requiring enough information to predict their responses under arbitrary sequences of both transformations splits the five classes into all 1,024 individual configurations.

## 1. The encounter was chosen independently of the orbit labels

I used

$$
\boxed{J(p,r)=(p,r+1\bmod16).}
$$

It increments the register and leaves the reader position unchanged. It is reversible:

$$
J^{-1}(p,r)=(p,r-1\bmod16).
$$

This encounter was declared before enumerating its effects in this run. It is a chosen test operation, not an encounter derived from the nested-ring geometry.

The reconstructed original transition again produced the five orbit sizes

$$
647,\quad191,\quad68,\quad95,\quad23,
$$

with no fixed configurations.

The accompanying executable and full transition table now expose the actual mapping used, rather than only its summary.

## 2. Identity fails both survival and quotient prediction

The exhaustive counts are:

| Test | Result |
|---|---:|
| $q_T(Jx)=q_T(x)$ | 474 configurations |
| $q_T(Jx)\ne q_T(x)$ | 550 configurations |
| Identity preserved everywhere | No |
| A well-defined map on the five identities | No |

Here is the complete encounter matrix. Each entry counts configurations in the source class that $J$ sends into the destination class. Class labels are ordered by their least encoded state, using $x=16p+r$.

| Source \ Destination | $C_0$ | $C_1$ | $C_2$ | $C_3$ | $C_4$ |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| $C_0$, size 647 | 424 | 116 | 29 | 58 | 20 |
| $C_1$, size 191 | 103 | 43 | 21 | 22 | 2 |
| $C_2$, size 68 | 48 | 9 | 2 | 9 | 0 |
| $C_3$, size 95 | 56 | 21 | 12 | 5 | 1 |
| $C_4$, size 23 | 16 | 2 | 4 | 1 | 0 |

Every source class has multiple possible destination classes. These are counts over configurations, not probabilities unless a distribution over configurations is specified.

### An explicit counterexample

Take

$$
x=(0,0),\qquad y=(40,1).
$$

They belong to the same original orbit:

$$
q_T(x)=q_T(y)=C_0.
$$

Indeed, $y=T^{13}(x)$. They differ only in where they lie along that orbit.

But

$$
Jx=(0,1)\in C_0,
\qquad
Jy=(40,2)\in C_2.
$$

Thus

$$
\boxed{
q_T(x)=q_T(y)
\quad\text{while}\quad
q_T(Jx)\ne q_T(Jy).
}
$$

The same persistent identity responds differently depending on its phase.

## 3. What survives both transformations?

I computed the equivalence relation generated by the connections

$$
x\leftrightarrow T(x),
\qquad
x\leftrightarrow J(x).
$$

All 1,024 configurations lie in one connected component.

Consequently, any observable $f$ required to satisfy both

$$
f(Tx)=f(x),
\qquad
f(Jx)=f(x)
$$

everywhere must be constant throughout this state space.

So the five identities are genuine invariants of $T$, but none supplies a nontrivial identity invariant under this enlarged encounter family.

That does not destroy the distinctions between configurations. It says that no nontrivial distinction stays unchanged under every allowed operation in this test.

## 4. What information predicts the encounters?

Separately, I started with the five orbit labels and repeatedly split classes whenever their members had different successor classes under $T$ or $J$:

$$
b_{n+1}(x)=
\operatorname{label}\!\left(
b_n(x),b_n(Tx),b_n(Jx)
\right),
\qquad b_0=q_T.
$$

This is a standard partition-refinement construction: retain the initial observations and distinguish states when their transition responses require it. [4086][4088]

The number of classes evolved as

$$
\boxed{
5\rightarrow23\rightarrow158\rightarrow626
\rightarrow951\rightarrow1011\rightarrow1024.
}
$$

The final partition consists entirely of singletons.

For this machine, predicting the original orbit-label readout under arbitrary finite words of $T$ and $J$ requires distinguishing every configuration. Equivalently, every pair of different configurations can be separated by some encounter sequence of length at most six.

This does not mean a single encounter needs the full state, or that every measurement must expose it. It means no smaller exact deterministic quotient retains all those possible future responses.

## 5. The boundary is now explicit

Your proposed distinction has an executed example:

$$
\begin{array}{rcl}
\text{identity unchanged under encounters}
&\Rightarrow& \text{coarsening: }5\to1,\\[2mm]
\text{identity sufficient to predict encounters}
&\Rightarrow& \text{refinement: }5\to1024.
\end{array}
$$

The five classes describe persistence under the original motion. They do not describe encounter-independent objects.

The full transition table, reproducible audit script, declaration record, and results accompany this answer. The demonstrated result concerns this specified reader and chosen $J$; deriving the encounter itself from the ring geometry remains a separate obligation.

the pointer/register machine on a fixed 64-symbol anchor. They are not the configuration space of the nested binary rings.

## 2. Test of the second operation

I reran every state using $G$, not the previous register increment $J$.

| Property | Executed result |
|---|---:|
| Configurations | 1,024 |
| $T$ bijective | Yes |
| $G$ bijective | Yes |
| $T$-orbit sizes | 647, 191, 68, 95, 23 |
| $G$-orbit structure | 16 cycles, each length 64 |
| States whose $T$-orbit identity survives $G$ | 489 |
| States whose $T$-orbit identity changes under $G$ | 535 |
| Does $G$ induce a deterministic map on the five identities? | No |

A particularly short witness is

$$
x=(0,0),\qquad y=T(x)=(4,3).
$$

Both belong to $C_0$. But

$$
Gx=(1,0)\in C_0,
\qquad
Gy=(5,3)\in C_1.
$$

Thus even adjacent phases of one persistent orbit respond differently to the same circular displacement.

## 3. The exact partitions where identity is invariant

Strictly, the transition does not “become invariant.” A reader’s class label becomes invariant under the transition.

There are three relevant partitions.

### Under $T$ alone: five blocks

Define

$$
C_i=\{T^k(a_i):0\le k<L_i\},
$$

using the least encoded state in each orbit as its representative, with state encoding $x=16p+r$.

Then

$$
\mathcal P_T=\{C_0,C_1,C_2,C_3,C_4\},
\qquad
(L_0,\ldots,L_4)=(647,191,68,95,23).
$$

This is the finest partition whose label remains unchanged under $T$:

$$
q_T(Tx)=q_T(x).
$$

The accompanying exact-partition record lists every member of every block in phase order.

### Under $G$ alone: sixteen blocks

Circular transport changes only the pointer. Its exact invariant partition is

$$
\boxed{
D_r=\{(p,r):p\in\mathbb Z_{64}\},
\qquad r=0,\ldots,15.
}
$$

The invariant is simply the register value:

$$
q_G(p,r)=r.
$$

Each block contains all 64 reader positions at one register value.

### Under both $T$ and $G$: one block

I computed the equivalence closure generated by

$$
x\sim T(x),
\qquad
x\sim G(x).
$$

It contains one component:

$$
\boxed{\mathcal P_{\mathrm{shared}}=\{S\}.}
$$

Therefore any observable satisfying

$$
f(Tx)=f(x)
\quad\text{and}\quad
f(Gx)=f(x)
$$

for every state must be constant.

This is the exact shared invariant partition—not merely an estimate from sampled routes.

## 4. What family of changes preserves the 1,024 configurations?

“Preserves” has two different meanings here.

### Preserves every distinction reversibly

The family

$$
\boxed{\mathcal H=\langle T,G\rangle}
$$

consists of finite compositions of $T,G$ and their inverses. Every member permutes the 1,024 configurations. No pair of distinct complete states merges.

The action is transitive: every configuration can reach every other through some composition of these operations and their inverses.

That establishes transitivity, not that $\mathcal H$ contains every possible permutation.

### Preserves each of the five identities

The largest reversible family satisfying

$$
q_T(Ax)=q_T(x)
$$

is the family of arbitrary permutations within each original orbit:

$$
\boxed{
\operatorname{Sym}(C_0)\times
\operatorname{Sym}(C_1)\times
\operatorname{Sym}(C_2)\times
\operatorname{Sym}(C_3)\times
\operatorname{Sym}(C_4).
}
$$

This preserves orbit membership but need not respect the order of motion.

If we additionally require $AT=TA$, then, because the five cycle lengths differ, $A$ can only rotate each cycle independently:

$$
\mathbb Z_{647}\times
\mathbb Z_{191}\times
\mathbb Z_{68}\times
\mathbb Z_{95}\times
\mathbb Z_{23}.
$$

That is the family preserving both orbit identity and its transition structure.

## 5. The coarser quotient structure

On the five-block quotient,

$$
\overline T(C_i)=C_i.
$$

But $G$ has no single-valued quotient map. Its exact class-to-class counts are:

| Source \ Destination | $C_0$ | $C_1$ | $C_2$ | $C_3$ | $C_4$ |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| $C_0$ | 436 | 103 | 40 | 58 | 10 |
| $C_1$ | 104 | 43 | 16 | 18 | 10 |
| $C_2$ | 32 | 17 | 7 | 11 | 1 |
| $C_3$ | 66 | 19 | 5 | 3 | 2 |
| $C_4$ | 9 | 9 | 0 | 5 | 0 |

This defines a quotient relation, not a deterministic quotient transition. A probability interpretation would require an additional distribution over the hidden configurations.

On the coarser shared quotient $\{S\}$, both operations become the identity on a single point. That quotient is exact but exposes no internal distinctions.

Conversely, retaining enough information to predict the original orbit-label observations under arbitrary $T/G$ encounters produces the refinement sequence

$$
5\rightarrow23\rightarrow192\rightarrow653
\rightarrow957\rightarrow1016\rightarrow1024.
$$

So **preservation** separates cleanly:

$$
\boxed{
\begin{aligned}
\text{Complete-state reversibility:}&\quad 1{,}024\text{ distinctions retained},\\
\text{Shared unchanging identity:}&\quad 1\text{ class},\\
\text{Exact predictive refinement:}&\quad 1{,}024\text{ classes}.
\end{aligned}
}
$$

The full transition table, exact orbit partition, results, and executable audit accompany this answer. No information disappears from the complete reversible system; it disappears only from the chosen quotient readout.

# perplexity response

I ran the next test using the nested-ring notebook’s actual construction. The result differs from the 1,024-state interpreter: geometry-derived angular moves merge the invariant classes into one, but they do not force the predictive reader to recover every position.

The reason is **commutation**: the two angular moves commute, so the original orbit quotient remains predictively sufficient.

## 1. Return to the actual ring construction

The notebook constructs each LEN ring using reversal-paired words, complementing palindromes and inserting pairs at the center. It does not concatenate the words in numerical order. Its nested display places those rings on a common angular coordinate. [4144]

That requires a correction to my earlier analysis: the numerical-order rotate-and-carry formula describes a different arrangement. It cannot be transferred unchanged to this notebook.

For the present test I used the doubling substack

$$
1\rightarrow2\rightarrow4\rightarrow8.
$$

The notebook also contains intermediate LENs; this test does not yet cover every adjacent pair.

## 2. Two operations supplied by the nested pitches

For an outer LEN $L$ and inner LEN $m$, their slice counts are

$$
N=L2^L,\qquad M=m2^m.
$$

When $M$ divides $N$, define

$$
f=\frac NM.
$$

An outer position decomposes exactly as

$$
\boxed{p=fi+r,\qquad 0\le r<f.}
$$

Here $i$ is the inner slice at the same angle, and $r$ is the offset within that inner slice.

The geometry supplies two elementary angular moves:

$$
\boxed{
A(p)=p+f\bmod N,
\qquad
B(p)=p+1\bmod N.
}
$$

- $A$ advances by one inner-slice pitch, retaining the within-slice offset.
- $B$ advances by one outer-slice pitch.

Neither operation consults the bit values, a gate table, or the previously computed orbit labels.

These are angular transport operations derived from the declared nested geometry. They are not yet bit-conditioned dynamics derived from the growth algorithm.

## 3. The invariant partition is explicit

Under $A$, the residue $r=p\bmod f$ never changes. Its exact orbit classes are

$$
\boxed{
C_r=\{r,r+f,r+2f,\ldots,r+(M-1)f\},
\quad r=0,\ldots,f-1.
}
$$

Each class has $M$ positions.

For LEN $2\rightarrow4$, $M=8$, $N=64$, and $f=8$. Thus, for example,

$$
C_0=\{0,8,16,24,32,40,48,56\},
$$

$$
C_1=\{1,9,17,25,33,41,49,57\}.
$$

The invariant reader is

$$
q_A(p)=p\bmod f.
$$

Now apply the second operation:

$$
q_A(Bp)=(q_A(p)+1)\bmod f.
$$

Unlike the earlier interpreter’s encounter, this has a well-defined action on identities. The destination class does not depend on the hidden position within the source class.

## 4. Exhaustive results

I enumerated every outer position for each tested pair.

| Inner → outer LEN | Outer positions | $A$-invariant classes | Positions per class | Shared invariant classes | Predictive classes retaining $q_A$ |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 1 → 2 | 8 | 4 | 2 | 1 | 4 |
| 2 → 4 | 64 | 8 | 8 | 1 | 8 |
| 4 → 8 | 2,048 | 32 | 64 | 1 | 32 |

Both operations are bijections, and every checked position satisfies

$$
A(Bp)=B(Ap).
$$

Indeed,

$$
A=B^f.
$$

They are two geometrically distinguished step sizes, but not algebraically independent generators.

That is why we do not reproduce the earlier predictive explosion.

## 5. What happens on the coarser quotient?

On the $f$-class quotient,

$$
\boxed{
\overline A(r)=r,\qquad
\overline B(r)=r+1\bmod f.
}
$$

This quotient is exactly the cyclic system $\mathbb Z_f$.

There are two different outcomes:

- If the label must remain unchanged under both operations, all classes merge: $B$ cycles through every residue.
- If the label may change but its next value must be predictable, the $f$-class quotient is already sufficient.

Thus

$$
\boxed{
\begin{aligned}
\text{Invariant under }A &: f\text{ classes},\\
\text{Invariant under }A,B &: 1\text{ class},\\
\text{Predictive resolution retaining }q_A &: f\text{ classes}.
\end{aligned}
}
$$

The hidden inner position $i$ is unnecessary for predicting this particular readout under any sequence of these moves.

It remains necessary if the requested output is the complete position—or potentially a bit readout that distinguishes positions inside these classes.

## 6. The actual bit arrangement exposes another correction

I also counted length-$L$ circular windows in the notebook’s generated rings.

| LEN | Distinct length-$L$ patterns | Occurrence counts |
|---|---:|---|
| 2 | 4 | Every pattern occurs twice |
| 4 | 16 | Counts range from 3 to 5 |
| 8 | 256 | Counts range from 6 to 10 |

So every possible word appears at these tested widths, but “every pattern occurs exactly $L$ times” is false for this arrangement.

That earlier uniformity result belonged to numerical-order concatenation, not the notebook’s center-insertion rule.

## What this establishes

The distinction between shared invariance and predictive sufficiency survives the move to the nested geometry. But full-state predictive necessity does not follow from nesting alone.

Here the geometry gives a nontrivial exact quotient:

$$
p\longmapsto p\bmod f,
$$

and both angular operations descend cleanly to it.

The next unresolved ingredient is therefore specific: whether the ring-growth relation or a bit-conditioned encounter induces an operation that fails to respect this quotient. Pure angular transport does not.

The executable audit, complete position table, and results accompany this answer.

# perplexity response

The phase-and-grouping map can be written exactly. For your source `00011011`, the clockwise/counterclockwise pairing holds at every reading width—not just at LEN 8. The nibble swap is a separate half-turn operation on the same circle.

That makes your point precise: the relationships live in the addresses and orientation before the readout is interpreted as binary, decimal, or hexadecimal.

## 1. The direction map is independent of LEN

Number the source positions $0,\ldots,7$:

```text
Position: 0 1 2 3 4 5 6 7
Bit:      0 0 0 1 1 0 1 1
```

Define the two reads by

$$
W_L^+(k)=s_k s_{k+1}\cdots s_{k+L-1},
$$

$$
W_L^-(k)=s_k s_{k-1}\cdots s_{k-L+1},
$$

with indices modulo eight.

This particular source satisfies the reflection identity

$$
\boxed{s_i=s_{2-i}.}
$$

Substituting that identity into the counterclockwise read gives

$$
s_{k-j}=s_{2-k+j}.
$$

Therefore

$$
\boxed{W_L^-(k)=W_L^+(2-k\bmod8).}
$$

The map changes the starting address; it does not invert the numerical value or complement the bits.

I checked every starting position for LEN 1–16. The identity passed throughout. The algebra establishes it for every positive LEN.

## 2. Where the three-position offset comes from

The exact offset depends on how the counterclockwise column enumerates its starting positions.

If row $t$ starts counterclockwise at position $7-t$, then

$$
W_L^-(7-t)=W_L^+(t+3).
$$

Thus the column follows the clockwise sequence shifted by three entries:

$$
\boxed{\text{counterclockwise row }t
=\text{clockwise row }t+3.}
$$

That produces the LEN-8 sequence beginning

```text
D8 → B1 → 63 → C6 → 8D → 1B → 36 → 6C
```

and the LEN-7 sequence beginning

```text
6C → 58 → 31 → 63 → 46 → 0D → 1B → 36
```

The same address rule explains both.

An important precision: if both columns instead advance their starting positions in the same direction, the correspondence is $k\mapsto2-k$, not a constant phase shift. The start-address convention is part of the operation.

## 3. The nibble swap comes from the half-turn

At LEN 8, each window spans the entire source circle. Advancing the starting address by four positions therefore exchanges its two four-bit halves:

$$
\boxed{
W_8^+(k+4)=\operatorname{ROL}_8(W_8^+(k),4).
}
$$

In hexadecimal:

| Original byte | Opposite starting address |
|---|---|
| `1B` | `B1` |
| `36` | `63` |
| `6C` | `C6` |
| `D8` | `8D` |

Consequently,

```text
1B · 36  →  B1 · 63
6C · D8  →  C6 · 8D
```

No nibble-swap instruction needs to be inserted. Hexadecimal makes the result visible because one hex digit represents four bits; the underlying operation is displacement by half the eight-position circle.

Applying it twice advances eight positions and returns to the starting address.

This does not mean that advancing four positions swaps the halves of every shorter window. At LEN 8, the reading width and source circumference coincide; that alignment is essential.

## 4. The address operations form one geometric system

Write

$$
R(k)=k+1\bmod8,
\qquad
F(k)=2-k\bmod8.
$$

Then

$$
R^8=I,\qquad F^2=I,\qquad FRF=R^{-1}.
$$

These are the rotation/reflection relations of a dihedral action: reflecting changes the orientation of rotation. [4146]

The half-turn is

$$
H=R^4.
$$

It obeys

$$
H^2=I,\qquad FH=HF.
$$

So the clockwise reads, reflected reads, and opposite-address nibble pairs are not disconnected numerical coincidences. They are readouts of related address operations on one source.

The **symmetry** belongs to this labeled source and its address relationships. Decimal or hexadecimal notation merely presents the resulting bit strings.

## 5. The shortest window that identifies the position

The executed counts reproduce the supplied table:

| LEN | Distinct windows among eight starts |
|---:|---:|
| 1 | 2 |
| 2 | 4 |
| 3 | 6 |
| 4 | 7 |
| 5 | 8 |
| 6 | 8 |
| 7 | 8 |
| 8 | 8 |

At LEN 4, positions 2 and 5 both read `0110`. One additional bit separates them:

$$
W_5^+(2)=01101,
\qquad
W_5^+(5)=01100.
$$

Thus LEN 5 is the minimum contiguous reading width that uniquely identifies all eight positions in this source.

That gives “floor” a concrete meaning here: a minimum resolving width for a specified reader. It is not yet a universal minimum width for every construction.

## 6. What the one-ring interpretation now supplies

For this worksheet, the single-ring interpretation is exact: all LEN rows are different-width readings of one eight-position circular source.

Two distinctions remain useful:

- The LEN-2 layer concatenates four two-bit words into eight positions. Those eight positions yield eight distinct full-length rotations; they do not represent eight independent newly created bits.
- The nested generator contains additional rings with their own word arrangements. Showing that all those rings are projections or sections of one larger ring still requires an explicit embedding map.

But the worksheet’s gap is now closed: direction reversal, the three-row offset, and the hexadecimal cross-pairs all follow from specified position maps. They do not require a decoder that knows the numerical pairs in advance.

# perplexity response

The first exact break occurs when an across-read keeps the inner-ring address but discards the offset within it. At the last outer slice inside that address, the next angular step crosses into a different inner slice.

The structure does not break there. The reduced cross-section stops being sufficient to predict its continuation. The missing distinction is the **carry**.

## 1. Trace the boundary directly

For nested rings with $N$ outer slices and $M$ inner slices, where $N=fM$, write

$$
p=fi+r,\qquad 0\le r<f.
$$

Here:

- $p$: outer position;
- $i$: inner position at the same angle;
- $r$: fine offset within that inner position.

One outer-slice step gives

$$
\boxed{
(i,r)\longmapsto
\left(
i+\mathbf1_{r=f-1}\bmod M,\;
r+1\bmod f
\right).
}
$$

Most steps change only the fine offset. The boundary is exactly

$$
\boxed{r=f-1.}
$$

At that position, the offset wraps to zero and the inner address advances.

### First concrete witness: LEN 2 inside LEN 4

The inner ring has eight slices; the outer has 64. Therefore $f=8$.

| Outer position | Across coordinates $(i,r)$ | After one outer step |
|---:|---|---|
| 0 | $(0,0)$ | $(0,1)$ |
| 6 | $(0,6)$ | $(0,7)$ |
| 7 | $(0,7)$ | $(1,0)$ |
| 8 | $(1,0)$ | $(1,1)$ |

Positions 0 and 7 both project to inner address 0. But their next inner addresses differ:

$$
\pi(0)=\pi(7)=0,
$$

$$
\pi(B0)=0,\qquad \pi(B7)=1.
$$

So there is no deterministic next-address rule based on $i$ alone.

Keeping $r$ closes the gap exactly. No additional opcode or history log is required.

## 2. The boundary repeats at every tested scale

I enumerated the doubling pairs from the notebook’s nested geometry. The notebook defines each LEN-$L$ ring with $L2^L$ bit slices. [4144]

| Inner → outer LEN | Fine slices per inner slice | First crossing | All crossing positions |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| 1 → 2 | 4 | 3 | $p\equiv3\pmod4$ |
| 2 → 4 | 8 | 7 | $p\equiv7\pmod8$ |
| 4 → 8 | 32 | 31 | $p\equiv31\pmod{32}$ |

This is a scale boundary: continuous circular transport becomes a conditional transition when read through a coarser layer.

It also separates two quotients we have used:

- Retaining $r=p\bmod f$ predicts its own next value perfectly.
- Retaining $i=\lfloor p/f\rfloor$ does not predict its next value without $r$.

Neither quotient is “the” cross-section independently of what we ask it to predict.

## 3. There is a second boundary: the palindrome seam

The notebook’s pairing rule is

$$
P(w)=
\begin{cases}
\operatorname{reverse}(w),&w\text{ is not palindromic},\\
\operatorname{complement}(w),&w\text{ is palindromic}.
\end{cases}
$$

Its center-insertion construction places partner words at reflected word addresses. [4144]

Under the corresponding bit-address reflection,

$$
p\longmapsto N-1-p,
$$

the two cases behave differently:

- For a nonpalindrome, word reversal and within-word reflection cancel: the bit survives unchanged.
- For a palindrome, the partner is its complement: the reflected bit flips.

Thus the pairing remains exact, but uniform value-preserving reflection fails precisely on the palindrome words.

The executed mismatch count is

$$
\boxed{L\,2^{\lceil L/2\rceil},}
$$

because there are $2^{\lceil L/2\rceil}$ palindromic $L$-bit words, each occupying $L$ slices.

For LEN 8, that is 128 reflected-bit mismatches among 2,048 positions. This is not random damage to the arrangement. It is the explicitly defined branch in the pairing rule.

## 4. Does the “one ring” representation itself break?

For any finite stack, it need not. A common angular grid can encode every layer exactly using

$$
K=\operatorname{lcm}(N_1,\ldots,N_L).
$$

Each of its angular cells carries the across-profile of the stack. But that cell must retain the full profile—or enough information to reconstruct it. Replacing it with one ordinary bit is a further compression, not an automatic consequence of nesting.

For an unbounded stack, no fixed finite $K$ can remain divisible by the unbounded slice counts. A finite common-grid representation must therefore refine, stop at a declared resolution, or change representation. That is a boundary of the finite encoding, not evidence that the underlying construction terminates.

The audit record accompanies this answer.

The sharp result is: the cross-section fails as a self-contained predictive state at the first discarded offset that can trigger a carry. The full geometry still has the route; the reduced reading no longer holds enough of it.

```python?code_reference&code_event_index=7
def partner(x):
    r = x[::-1]
    return r if r != x else "".join("1" if c == "0" else "0" for c in x)

def grow(L):
    seen, pairs = set(), []
    for w in range(1 << L):
        x = format(w, f"0{L}b")
        if x in seen: continue
        p = partner(x); seen |= {x, p}; pairs.append((x, p))
    seq = []
    for i, (a, b) in enumerate(pairs):
        if i == len(pairs) - 1 and len(pairs) > 1: a, b = b, a
        m = len(seq) // 2
        seq = seq[:m] + [a, b] + seq[m:]
    return seq

for L in range(1, 5):
    g = grow(L)
    bits = "".join(g)
    print(f"L={L} ({len(g)} words, {len(bits)} bits): {g}")
    print(f"   concatenated bits: {bits}")

# Check rotation of grow(2):
# grow(2) is ['00', '10', '01', '11'] -> concatenated: '00100111' (which is "Circle as drawn, clockwise from top" in two_bit_circle.xlsx row 1!)
# And reversed + complemented (or read counter-clockwise): '00011011'!
g2_bits = "".join(grow(2))
print("\ngrow(2) bits:", g2_bits)
print("two_bit_circle.xlsx Row 1 'Circle as drawn, clockwise from top': 00100111")
print("Bitwise complement of reversed grow(2) bits:", "".join("1" if c == "0" else "0" for c in g2_bits[::-1]))

```

To put the concrete mathematics and code in your four uploaded files to work without skimming, below is the complete technical breakdown of the circular sequence workbook (`two_bit_circle.xlsx`), the nested-ring generator (`partner` and `grow`), and the configuration-space cohomology results.

---

### 1. Exact Analysis of `two_bit_circle.xlsx` and `Summarize-Circular-Sequence`

The spreadsheet `two_bit_circle.xlsx` models a single 8-bit circular source string across sample window sizes $L \in \{2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8\}$. It reconciles two representations of the length-2 ring:
* **Drawn Clockwise Ring (`00100111`)**: Reading the four quadrants clockwise from the top-right gives `TR = 00` (slices 1–2), `BR = 10` (slices 3–4), `BL = 01` (slices 5–6), and `LT = 11` (slices 7–8), which concatenates to `00100111`. This is the exact output of `grow(2)` (`['00', '10', '01', '11']`) in the notebook code.
* **Sheet Sampled Cycle (`00011011`)**: Reading the drawn circle in reverse orientation with `0` and `1` complemented (or concatenating `00, 01, 10, 11` in ascending order) yields the 8-slice source $S = \texttt{00011011}$. In the spreadsheet's `Not` column at $L=8$, row $k=3$ (`W_8(3) = 11011000`, decimal `216`, hex `D8`) has bitwise complement `00100111`, linking the sheet's sampled cycle back to the drawn clockwise ring.

#### Window Recurrence, Phase Offset, and Summary Statistics ($L = 2 \dots 8$)
For any sample width $L$ and starting index $k \in \{0, \dots, 7\}$, the clockwise window $W_L^+(k) = s_k s_{k+1}\dots s_{k+L-1} \pmod 8$ obeys the positional extension and overlap laws:

$$W_{L+1}(k) = 2W_L(k) + s_{(k+L)\bmod 8}, \qquad W_{L+1}(k) \bmod 2^L = W_L(k+1 \bmod 8)$$

Because $S = \texttt{00011011}$ is reflection-symmetric about index $1$ ($s_i = s_{(2-i)\bmod 8}$), a true counterclockwise window $W_L^-(k) = s_k s_{k-1}\dots s_{k-L+1}$ equals the clockwise window $W_L^+(2-k \bmod 8)$ at every width $L$. In `two_bit_circle.xlsx`, the "Counter Clockwise" column is implemented via the formula `=MID(REPT($B$9, L+2), MOD(row-1+3, 8)+1, L)`, which applies a constant phase offset of $+3$ slices ($W_L^+(k+3 \bmod 8)$) so the first entry lands on `11`.

* **Resolution Floor at $L=5$**: At $L=4$, slices $k=2$ and $k=5$ both read `0110` (decimal 6), giving 7 distinct windows. At $L=5$, they separate into `01101` (13) and `01100` (12), making $L=5$ the minimum contiguous window width that uniquely resolves all 8 positions of $S$.
* **Exact Mean Scaling**: Because $S$ has exactly four `1`s and four `0`s, every bit position across the 8 windows is `1` four times and `0` four times. As a result, the mean decimal value at width $L$ is $\frac{1}{2}(2^L - 1)$, which gives $1.5, 3.5, 7.5, 15.5, 31.5, 63.5, 127.5$ for $L=2,\dots,8$.

### 2. The 8-Bit Half-Turn Pairs and Residual Structure

At the end of `Summarize-Circular-Sequence-_2026_10_19__0237.md`, you pointed out that averaging all 8 phases at $L=8$ cancels the structure into a flat 50/50 occupancy, whereas grouping the 8-bit orbit into **4 half-turn pairs** preserves the orientation residual.

At $L=8$, a half-turn around the 8-slice circle ($k \mapsto k+4 \bmod 8$) is a 4-bit circular rotation $\operatorname{ROL}_8(W_8(k), 4)$, which swaps the high and low hexadecimal nibbles of each byte:
* **Pair 0 ($k=0, 4$)**: $(\texttt{1B}, \texttt{B1}) = (27, 177)$ in decimal, or `(00011011, 10110001)` in binary.
* **Pair 1 ($k=1, 5$)**: $(\texttt{36}, \texttt{63}) = (54, 99)$ in decimal, or `(00110110, 01100011)` in binary.

### 3. Nested-Ring Generator (`grow(L)`) and Cross-Section Boundaries

In `nested_rings (3).ipynb`, the ring of word length $L$ is constructed by pairing each $L$-bit word $x$ with its involution partner:

$$\operatorname{partner}(x) = \begin{cases} \operatorname{reverse}(x) & \text{if } x \neq \operatorname{reverse}(x) \\ \operatorname{complement}(x) & \text{if } x = \operatorname{reverse}(x) \end{cases}$$

Inserting each pair at the center of the list (and swapping the final pair) guarantees the global mirror invariant across all $2^L$ words:

$$\operatorname{partner}(\text{ring}[i]) = \text{ring}[2^L - 1 - i] \quad \forall i \in \{0, \dots, 2^L - 1\}$$

* **De Bruijn Pathway Graph vs. Center-Insertion Address**: Every $L$-bit word $w_1\dots w_L$ defines a directed edge from its $(L-1)$-bit prefix $w_1\dots w_{L-1}$ to its $(L-1)$-bit suffix $w_2\dots w_L$ on the ring below it, giving each $(L-1)$-bit word in-degree 2 and out-degree 2.
* **Where the Cross-Section Breaks**: When nesting an inner ring of $M = m2^m$ slices inside an outer ring of $N = L2^L$ slices (with ratio $f = N/M$), every outer slice $p$ has coordinates $(i, r)$ where $i = \lfloor p/f \rfloor$ is the inner slice address and $r = p \bmod f$ is the within-slice offset. Under a 1-slice outer step $B(p) = p+1 \bmod N$, the coordinates update as $(i, r) \mapsto (i + \mathbf{1}_{r=f-1} \bmod M, \, r+1 \bmod f)$. Thus, if a cross-section reader only retains the coarse inner address $i$, deterministic prediction breaks specifically at the **carry boundary $r = f - 1$**. Furthermore, under bit-level reflection $p \mapsto N - 1 - p$, non-palindromic words preserve their bit values because word-order reversal and within-word bit reversal cancel, whereas palindromic words flip their bit values because `partner(w)` complements palindromes—producing $L\,2^{\lceil L/2 \rceil}$ seam flips on ring $L$.

```python?code_reference&code_event_index=2
def partner(x):
    r = x[::-1]
    return r if r != x else "".join("1" if c == "0" else "0" for c in x)

def grow(L):
    seen, pairs = set(), []
    for w in range(1 << L):
        x = format(w, f"0{L}b")
        if x in seen: continue
        p = partner(x); seen |= {x, p}; pairs.append((x, p))
    seq = []
    for i, (a, b) in enumerate(pairs):
        if i == len(pairs) - 1 and len(pairs) > 1: a, b = b, a
        m = len(seq) // 2
        seq = seq[:m] + [a, b] + seq[m:]
    return seq

for L in range(1, 7):
    ring = grow(L)
    bits = "".join(ring)
    # Count palindromes vs non-palindromes
    pals = [format(w, f"0{L}b") for w in range(1 << L) if format(w, f"0{L}b") == format(w, f"0{L}b")[::-1]]
    print(f"L={L}: {len(ring)} words, {len(bits)} slices, {len(pals)} palindromes ({len(pals)//2} complement pairs)")
    if L <= 4:
        print("  Ring:", ring)
        print("  Bits:", bits)
        # Check sliding windows of length L
        windows = [ (bits + bits[:L-1])[i:i+L] for i in range(len(bits)) ]
        unique_windows = set(windows)
        print(f"  Unique L-bit sliding windows across ring: {len(unique_windows)} / {1<<L}")
    else:
        windows = [ (bits + bits[:L-1])[i:i+L] for i in range(len(bits)) ]
        unique_windows = set(windows)
        print(f"  Unique L-bit sliding windows across ring: {len(unique_windows)} / {1<<L}")

```

## 1. The Center-Out Ring Compiler (`grow(L)`) & `two_bit_circle`

The notebook code (`grow(L)` and `partner(x)`) implements the **center-out reflected wrapper** ("building from the center out in reflection", where `(` and `)` are placed simultaneously around the seam):

* **The Partner Involution (`partner(x)`):** Every $L$-bit word $x \in \{0,1\}^L$ is paired across the fold. If $x$ is non-palindromic ($x \neq x^R$), its partner is its bit-reversal $x^R$ (spatial/directional reflection). If $x$ is a palindrome ($x = x^R$), bit-reversal fixes it, so it would collide with itself; to obey **C0/C1** (no fixed point / cannot hold still), it takes its bitwise complement $\bar{x}$ (value inversion).
  * For any bit-length $L$, there are $2^{\lfloor(L+1)/2\rfloor}$ palindromes (forming $2^{\lfloor(L-1)/2\rfloor}$ complement pairs) and $2^L - 2^{\lfloor(L+1)/2\rfloor}$ non-palindromes (forming $2^{L-1} - 2^{\lfloor(L-1)/2\rfloor}$ reversal pairs), partitioning $\{0,1\}^L$ into $2^{L-1}$ disjoint pairs.
* **Center-Out Nesting (`grow(L)`):** Pairs are generated in lexicographical order of their first unseen representative. Starting with the poles `(0...0, 1...1)`, each subsequent pair `(a, b)` is spliced directly into the midpoint $m = \text{len}(\text{seq}) // 2$, pushing earlier pairs outward toward the boundaries. On the final pair ($i = 2^{L-1} - 1$), the orientation is twisted (`a, b = b, a`) to prevent a mirror-stall at the innermost seam.
  * **At $L=2$:** Pairs `(00, 11)` and `(01, 10)` twist at the center to yield `['00', '10', '01', '11']` $\to$ bit-ring `00100111` (8 slices).
  * **At $L=3$:** Yields `['000', '001', '010', '110', '011', '101', '100', '111']` (8 words, 24 slices, matching the 24-state witness walk).
  * **At $L=6$:** Yields 64 words and 384 slices, starting with `000000` at the top clockwise boundary, twisting `(101111, 111011)` at indices 30–31, and closing on `111111` at index 63.

print("Sheets:", xls.sheet_names)
for s in xls.sheet_names:
    print(f"=== {s} ===")
    df = pd.read_excel('two_bit_circle.xlsx', sheet_name=s)
    print(df.to_string())

```

```python?code_reference&code_event_index=2
import openpyxl

wb = openpyxl.load_workbook('two_bit_circle.xlsx', data_only=False)
ws = wb['2-bit circle']
for row in ws.iter_rows(min_row=1, max_row=30, values_only=False):
    vals = [c.value for c in row if c.value is not None]
    if vals:
        print(f"Row {row[0].row}: {vals[:10]}")

```

for col_idx in range(1, 18):
    col_letter = openpyxl.utils.get_column_letter(col_idx)
    print(f"{col_letter}14: {ws.cell(14, col_idx).value} | {col_letter}15: {ws.cell(15, col_idx).value} | {col_letter}16: {ws.cell(16, col_idx).value}")

G14: Dec | G15: =BIN2DEC(F15) | G16: =BIN2DEC(F16)
H14: Hex | H15: =BIN2HEX(F15) | H16: =BIN2HEX(F16)
I14: Oct | I15: =BIN2OCT(F15) | I16: =BIN2OCT(F16)
J14: Hex nib | J15: None | J16: =BIN2HEX(F16)&BIN2HEX(F17)
K14: Not | K15: =SUBSTITUTE(SUBSTITUTE(SUBSTITUTE(A15,"1","x"),"0","1"),"x","0") | K16: =SUBSTITUTE(SUBSTITUTE(SUBSTITUTE(A16,"1","x"),"0","1"),"x","0")
L14: And | L15: =IF((LEN(A15)-LEN(SUBSTITUTE(A15,"1","")))=LEN(A15),1,0) | L16: =IF((LEN(A16)-LEN(SUBSTITUTE(A16,"1","")))=LEN(A16),1,0)
M14: Nand | M15: =IF((LEN(A15)-LEN(SUBSTITUTE(A15,"1","")))=LEN(A15),0,1) | M16: =IF((LEN(A16)-LEN(SUBSTITUTE(A16,"1","")))=LEN(A16),0,1)
N14: Or | N15: =IF((LEN(A15)-LEN(SUBSTITUTE(A15,"1","")))>0,1,0) | N16: =IF((LEN(A16)-LEN(SUBSTITUTE(A16,"1","")))>0,1,0)
O14: Nor | O15: =IF((LEN(A15)-LEN(SUBSTITUTE(A15,"1","")))=0,1,0) | O16: =IF((LEN(A16)-LEN(SUBSTITUTE(A16,"1","")))=0,1,0)
P14: Xor | P15: =MOD((LEN(A15)-LEN(SUBSTITUTE(A15,"1",""))),2) | P16: =MOD((LEN(A16)-LEN(SUBSTITUTE(A16,"1",""))),2)
Q14: Xnor | Q15: =IF(MOD((LEN(A15)-LEN(SUBSTITUTE(A15,"1",""))),2)=0,1,0) | Q16: =IF(MOD((LEN(A16)-LEN(SUBSTITUTE(A16,"1",""))),2)=0,1,0)

```



```python?code_reference&code_event_index=4
def partner(x):
    r = x[::-1]
    return r if r != x else "".join("1" if c == "0" else "0" for c in x)

def grow(L):
    seen, pairs = set(), []
    for w in range(1 << L):
        x = format(w, f"0{L}b")
        if x in seen: continue
        p = partner(x); seen |= {x, p}; pairs.append((x, p))
    seq = []
    for i, (a, b) in enumerate(pairs):
        if i == len(pairs) - 1 and len(pairs) > 1: a, b = b, a
        m = len(seq) // 2
        seq = seq[:m] + [a, b] + seq[m:]
    return seq

for L in range(1, 7):
    ring = grow(L)
    drawn = "".join(ring)
    sheet = "".join("1" if c == "0" else "0" for c in drawn[::-1])
    N = len(drawn)
    # Check if sheet is a cyclic shift of drawn!
    doubled = drawn + drawn
    idx_in_drawn = doubled.find(sheet)
    print(f"L={L} (N={N}): sheet in drawn? index={idx_in_drawn}")

```

```python?code_reference&code_event_index=5
for L in range(1, 7):
    ring = grow(L)
    drawn = "".join(ring)
    rev = drawn[::-1]
    comp = "".join("1" if c == "0" else "0" for c in drawn)
    rev_comp = "".join("1" if c == "0" else "0" for c in rev)
    d2 = drawn + drawn
    print(f"L={L} (N={len(drawn)}): rev_idx={d2.find(rev)}, comp_idx={d2.find(comp)}, rev_comp_idx={d2.find(rev_comp)}")
    # Let's check word-level symmetries!
    w_rev = ring[::-1]
    w_comp = ["".join("1" if c == "0" else "0" for c in w) for w in ring]
    w_part = [partner(w) for w in ring]
    print(f"   word-level: ring[::-1] == [partner(w) for w in ring]? {w_rev == w_part}")
    # Wait: why wasn't it true for all words? Because the last pair was swapped!
    diffs = [(i, ring[len(ring)-1-i], partner(ring[i])) for i in range(len(ring)) if ring[len(ring)-1-i] != partner(ring[i])]
    print(f"   word-level diffs from exact partner-symmetry: {len(diffs)} words -> indices {[d[0] for d in diffs]}")

```

```python?code_reference&code_event_index=6
from collections import Counter

for L in range(1, 9):
    ring = grow(L)
    drawn = "".join(ring)
    sheet = "".join("1" if c == "0" else "0" for c in drawn[::-1])
    N = len(drawn)
    d_ext = drawn + drawn[:L-1]
    s_ext = sheet + sheet[:L-1]
    w_drawn = [d_ext[i:i+L] for i in range(N)]
    w_sheet = [s_ext[i:i+L] for i in range(N)]
    c_drawn = Counter(w_drawn)
    c_sheet = Counter(w_sheet)
    print(f"L={L} (2^L={1<<L}, N={N}):")
    print(f"  drawn coverage: {len(c_drawn)}/{1<<L} | min_freq={min(c_drawn.values())}, max_freq={max(c_drawn.values())}")
    print(f"  sheet coverage: {len(c_sheet)}/{1<<L} | min_freq={min(c_sheet.values())}, max_freq={max(c_sheet.values())}")
    # Also check L+1 bit windows!
    d_ext1 = drawn + drawn[:L]
    w_drawn1 = [d_ext1[i:i+L+1] for i in range(N)]
    print(f"  (L+1)-bit transitions covered in drawn: {len(set(w_drawn1))}/{1<<(L+1)}")

```

for L in range(1, 9):
    ring = grow(L)
    drawn = "".join(ring)
    N = len(drawn)
    d_ext1 = drawn + drawn[:L]
    present = set(d_ext1[i:i+L+1] for i in range(N))
    all_trans = set(format(w, f"0{L+1}b") for w in range(1 << (L+1)))
    missing = sorted(all_trans - present)
    print(f"L={L}: missing {len(missing)}/{1<<(L+1)} transitions:")
    if len(missing) <= 24:
        print("  ", missing)
    else:
        print("  ", missing[:10], "...", missing[-10:])

import time

for L in range(1, 13):
    t0 = time.time()
    ring = grow(L)
    bits = "".join(ring)
    N = len(bits)
    b_ext = bits + bits[:L-1]
    w_set = set(b_ext[i:i+L] for i in range(N))
    b_ext1 = bits + bits[:L]
    w1_set = set(b_ext1[i:i+L+1] for i in range(N))
    dt = time.time() - t0
    print(f"L={L:2d} | words={1<<L:6d} | slices={N:7d} | L-win: {len(w_set):6d}/{1<<L:6d} | (L+1)-trans: {len(w1_set):6d}/{1<<(L+1):6d} (missing { (1<<(L+1)) - len(w1_set):5d}) | {dt:.3f}s")

for L in range(1, 9):
    ring = grow(L)
    drawn = "".join(ring)
    N = len(drawn)
    d_ext1 = drawn + drawn[:L]
    present = set(d_ext1[i:i+L+1] for i in range(N))
    all_trans = set(format(w, f"0{L+1}b") for w in range(1 << (L+1)))
    missing = sorted(all_trans - present)
    # Check if missing is closed under reversal, complement, or partner!
    m_set = set(missing)
    rev_closed = all(m[::-1] in m_set for m in m_set)
    comp_closed = all("".join("1" if c=="0" else "0" for c in m) in m_set for m in m_set)
    part_closed = all(partner(m) in m_set for m in m_set)
    print(f"L={L:2d}: missing={len(missing):3d} | rev_closed={rev_closed} | comp_closed={comp_closed} | partner_closed={part_closed}")

```

for L in range(1, 9):
    ring = grow(L)
    drawn = "".join(ring)
    N = len(drawn)
    d_ext1 = drawn + drawn[:L]
    present = set(d_ext1[i:i+L+1] for i in range(N))
    all_trans = set(format(w, f"0{L+1}b") for w in range(1 << (L+1)))
    missing = sorted(all_trans - present)
    print(f"=== L={L} (missing {len(missing)}) ===")
    for m in missing[:12]:
        u, v = m[:-1], m[1:]
        pos_u = [i for i, w in enumerate(ring) if w == u][0]
        pos_v = [i for i, w in enumerate(ring) if w == v][0]
        # Also where do u and v appear as sliding windows?
        d_ext = drawn + drawn[:L-1]
        win_u = [(i, i//L, i%L) for i in range(N) if d_ext[i:i+L] == u]
        print(f"  {m} ({u} -> {v}): word_idx=({pos_u}, {pos_v}), u_windows(slice, word, offset)={win_u}")

```
